=== FILE: ACE CERTIFIED ｜ Exercise Programming for Clients Who Have Fibromyalgia [dwkFHy_b0nM].txt ===

Hi, my name's Lance Dallek. I'm professor of exercise and sports science at Western Colorado University, and also a member of the American Council on Exercise's scientific advisory panel. In this month's issue of Certified, my colleague, Angela Dallek, and I have authored an article on exercise programming considerations for clients with fibromyalgia. With many of the past articles over the last 18 months on exercise programming considerations for clients with chronic conditions, generally speaking, the exercise programming in terms of cardiorespiratory fitness, muscular trening are very comparable, and we can follow the ACE integrated fitness trening model and guidelines, but that said, there's a few unique considerations for this group of individuals with fibromyalgia that are really quite critical, and I wanna highlight some of those in my video segment today, and it's also touched on in the article. So there's really three things I'm always trying to accomplish with clients that I've worked with with fibromyalgia. First is to try to educate them on the evidence base that exercise can be really beneficial for their condition, in particular, managing their symptoms of pain and fatigue, and really to prioritize over the first few weeks building trust with that client, and by this IMean if they've been inactive and we're introducing structured and regular exercise, there could be a trensient increase in their pain, in their fatigue, but that should be very subtle, and then over a few week period it should get better, and so first educating them on what to expect, then correctly giving them the right amount of exercise, the right intensity of exercise, so they see that maybe slight uptick in those symptoms for several days, but then really a very pronounced and gradual improvement, you're gonna earn their trust, and they're gonna be a lot easier to work with in the weeks and months moving forward. A second priority that I have is to really, and it's kind of linked with what I've just mentioned, it's building trust, it's progressing slowly. Most of us know, as exercise professionals, that there's a delayed reaction to what we do in terms of exercise, so if we overdo it, we're not sore immediately, it's two or three days later that we're sore. This physiological response in reality is even more pronounced with clients with fibromyalgia, so it's quite important to, if there is any improvement in an increase in weight, an increase in exercise time, or intensity, that it's very, very gradual, and then you're patient to see, well, what is their reaction to that, so that you don't really just, you don't push them over the edge, so to speak. And then third is, because of the pathophysiology of fibromyalgia, that the symptoms are extremely variable from day to day, and so it's quite important to always check in with these clients at the beginning, check in how they're doing, if they're having a flare-up, if certain symptoms arEQuite worse that day relative to how they were just maybe 48 hours earlier in the week, because sometimes, again, as exercise professionals, we'll get a routine set up for our clients, and it's really something that they can stick to, and there's not adjustments that need to be made on a day-to-day basis. It's more after a month or two months where we're making adjustments to exercise programming considerations, and so that's not the case with fibromyalgia. With fibromyalgia, it's day-to-day. Their symptoms may vary, and our exercise programming for that day has to match what they can do. Really, really critical. And then there's one other thing that I found very, very interesting and very important to be mindful of with individuals with fibromyalgia, and that's that they don't tolerate extremes in temperature from the hot end down to the cold end. So if it's really hot out, it can exacerbate their fatigue, for example, or if it's really cold out, it can cause them to have increased severity of their pain. And so checking in on that, checking in how they're doing. Earlier in my career, I lived in Wisconsin, and Wisconsin is notorious for having cold winters, but then in the summertime, it can be triple digits and humid. And so if your client with fibromyalgia is doing something outside, you might have to adjust the timing of that. If the gym tends to get hot over the course of the day, you might have to adjust when they're doing exercise. And so really, really unique consideration. As humans, naturally, our physiology is impacted by extremes in temperature, hot and cold, but more pronounced with clients with fibromyalgia, and so something to be mindful of as exercise professionals when you're working with your clients with fibromyalgia. So as always, it's wonderful to talk with you. I'll see you next time, and good luck with your clients.

One strategy we've employed very successfully when working with individuals with stroke and their comorbidities is not to start with the most serious condition, but one that's the most limiting. As an example, individual clients with stroke sometimes, actually very commonly, have problems with deconditioning, poor strength, poor mobility, and so we like to start there. We like to start with low-intensity resistance trening, range of motion, improving posture and strength, and what this tends to do is it unlocks the potential for this individual recovering from stroke to then do regular cardiorespiratory exercise sessions. And as a result of that, they can then see things like blood pressure and blood glucose control be more easily managed. So another consideration or another benefit to this strategy is that these clients don't just see what they can't do, they can really see the progress. So they can see, ah, my posture's getting better, I am getting a little stronger, I can do 5 minutes and 10 minutes and 20 minutes of regular cardiorespiratory exercise. And that can be really encouraging for clients' recovery from stroke and really help them with their overall quality of life, and again, with managing those comorbidities. And then the second item I want to talk about in today's video that's also in the article is that I have found that individuals recovering from stroke really require more careful supervision and monitoring than even other chronic conditions that I've previously written about and certified. The risk of recurrent stroke can bEQuite high, especially in the first six months post-stroke. So it's important for health and exercise professionals to be able to recognize symptoms, such as problems with vision, problems with recognition, they're having really obvious problems thinking, there's numbness or tingling in the extremities. And so being aware of these warning signs of a stroke in your clients is really quite critical. And then depending on the part of the brain that was impacted by the stroke, it's also been my experience that these clients can have problems with cognitive functioning. And as a result of that, we just need to be more mindful and more aware and supervise them more closely when we're having them go through the exercise routine. And we might have different settings on a piece of exercise equipment, such as a recumbent bike, and we just need to make sure that they're performing the right resistance and they have the right settings for proper full range of motion of their legs on the recumbent bike exercise. And same thing with machine resistance trening exercise. Are they doing the right weights? And it's something that maybe we take for granted with other clients we work with and we need to be a little bit more careful about. So I wanted to conclude with also some comments on even though, as with many chronic conditions, clients post-stroke can be challenging, it's highly rewarding. And as I alluded to earlier, just starting with small rate limiting issues and making some progress with this can be really, really impactful for your clients and really boost their confidence. And then long term, their overall quality of life is better because they're more conditioned and they're more able to function post-stroke than they would have been without working with you as a health and exercise professional. So as always, great to talk with you about another chronic condition. This month it's stroke. As always, good luck with your clients and we'll talk to you next time. Thank you.

So synapses are those connections between your neurons, the brain cells when you are forming a new habit, and as you continue to practice it, those synapses get stronger. So those connections get stronger. That's how the habit is formed, because as that those synapses get stronger, that habit, that action, that behavior becomes more automatic. When you have habits that you haven't practiced for a while, the brain will actually go through and prune it. And that's called synaptic pruning. It's called habit stacking. And maybe you already do this and you've heard of it. But it's just another way to instead of trying to form these new habits, you are still trying to form the habit, but you're connecting it to a habit that is already well established. So, for example, let's say that you or your client wants to add more leg work, OK, hip and leg work into your routine. One way to do that is to connect it to a habit. Another one might be, let's say you want to start a routine of reading at bedtime. So you already have an established routine and habit of making your bed in the morning. So one way to do that would be to take a book and place it on your pillow. So that way at bed, you already have you reminded that you're going to read that night. So you're stacking that habit with making your bed in the morning. Here's another one. Let's say you have your habit of brushing your teeth, hopefully. And so you want to start a habit of meditating. So you can stack that with brushing your teeth. So your trigger is, so when I brush my teeth, I will then just kind of pause and do one minute of meditation. So this is just another way of using those triggers that we talked about in the article and stacking those with your habits that you want to form. So I hope that helps. In my video segment, I want to briefly explain, as I do in the article, the differences between these three categories of prevention. Then I also want to highlight a couple of the evidence-based strategies that I've put in this month's article. Before that, I do want to say I'm really excited about writing this article because if I go back 25 years, my first professional position was in cardiac rehabilitation. Heart disease prevention is really near and dear to me. What are the three different categories of prevention? Primordial prevention means we're simply avoiding risk factors such as high blood pressure or high blood glucose from ever developing. Primary prevention means that over the course of screening, a risk factor has been identified and then we bring in strategies to try to modify positively that risk factor. And then secondary prevention means that unfortunately someone has a clinical type of event such as a heart attack. In the course of their follow-up treatment from that, various risk factors are identified and then a program or a strategy is incorporated to modify, again positively, those risk factors. In a perfect world, primordial prevention would be what we practice, starting with kids and young adults and adopting healthy lifestyles that ensure that the leading risk factors for heart disease, such as low cardiorespiratory fitness or high blood pressure or obesity, never in fact develop. And the reason for that is primordial prevention has been shown to be so powerful and so effective in terms of reducing the risk for cardiovascular disease developing or mortality from cardiovascular disease. So one of the studies that I want to share with you, it's one of my favorite studies of all time, goes back to about 2005-2006. A group of researchers, and they took this data from the Framingham study, which is an ongoing study in subsequent generations where they've looked at these people over the course of their lifetime and done annual surveys, forms of data collection, and look at heart endpoints such as developing different chronic diseases, mortality from different chronic diseases. And so in this particular study, what they found that is if individuals could reach 50 risk factor free, that means when they turn 50, which I did earlier this year, when they turn 50 they had no risk factors for heart disease. Those individuals had a really low chance of developing heart disease in their lifetime. About 5% for men and 8% for women. The study also showed that people that reached 50 risk factor free lived 8 to 10 years longer. So really, really cool study and really powerful statistics that highlight the importance of primordial prevention. A second strategy that I outline in my paper this month is for secondary prevention. And going back to, as I shared with you, being in cardiac rehabilitation early in my career, I oftentimes had clients ask me, what do I have to do to stop or reverse the process of heart disease? And I didn't know. Well, in the progress or in the process of doing my research to answer this question for my clients, I came across this really awesome study going back to the 90s by a group of researchers from Germany. And what they did is they took a group of individuals with diagnosed heart disease and they did an angiogram. Well, they went in with a catheter and determined how much plaque had built up in the main coronary arteries. And then these individuals did rehabilitation for a year. And then they went back in again with an angiogram to determine the severity, the progress or the stabilization or the reversal of their atherosclerosis, their plaque. And interestingly, they showed that if individuals did enough exercise that they could actually stabilize the plaque, that it got no worse. And more compellingly, they showed that if they did a pretty high volume of exercise, about six hours a week or 2200 calories per week of exercise, that plaque actually reversed. So this was this is a really, really cool study because it shows that even if we have sins early in our life with regard to our health, and we do unfortunately develop cardiovascular disease and having events, that we can still change that if we do enough of a lifestyle change in terms of the physical activity or exercise. So there's several additional studies that I highlight that give give you as certified professionals, different strategies for for primordial primary and secondary prevention in this article. And as I've done today, I've just shared with you a couple of my favorites, but there's some other really good ones in there. So as always, it's a pleasure talking to you and good luck with your clients and enjoy American Heart Month. Take care.

 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 22 on high-intensity functional training. High-intensity functional training or HIFT involves a combination of cardiorespiratory and muscular training exercises performed at high intensity and HIFT can be implemented several different ways as an accessory work for strength athletes, as cross training for endurance athletes, or also a standalone exercise training. There's really three key takeaway messages from this month's article. First is HIFT is effective. We already know from research that high-intensity interval training is as good as or superior to continuous moderate intensity training, but HIFT tends to be more time efficient. So if we combine HIFT with muscular training, we get augmented benefits and we get benefits in a number of different important fitness parameters including cardiorespiratory fitness, muscular strength, as well as anaerobic power. The second key takeaway is that HIFT is safe. Injury incident rates of HIFT are comparable, actually in most instances, lower than other forms of training such as weight training, power lifting, and running. And then the third key takeaway is that research has demonstrated that HIFT is enjoyable. And that's really critical and something we're learning a lot more about as a research community and fitness professionals is that we can have a form of training that is effective, we can afford have a form of training that is safe, but it also needs to be enjoyable. And HIFT has been demonstrated to be enjoyable. It tends to be done in a group setting and that provides, produces or elicits a form of community. And it's also been when compared to moderate intensity training, which is typically recommended, it's also been found to be more enjoyable. HIFT has been found more to be more enjoyable than that. The other highlights of this article include a number of great example HIFT workouts, as well as tips for how to implement into training and to progress training. And I really want to once again acknowledge my graduate student Michael Braun. He's the expert on HIFT and he's the one who has provided these sample workouts and the tips for progression as well as implementation. And please feel free to reach out and I can put you in contact with Michael Braun. I also wanted to wrap up by highlighting that we're performing research in our high altitude performance lab on the effectiveness of HIFT in a lot of different areas including cardiometabolic benefits, benefits to post-exercise oxygen consumption, and for a look for those findings to be highlighted in the future. As always good luck with your clients and enjoy your holidays.

First, we're going to really do a good job with training recovery for our older adult clients. We need to appreciate the physiology of aging. As we get older, unfortunately, our fitness decreases. Cardiorespiratory fitness and muscular fitness. Underpinning these decreases in fitness is a decrease in muscle mass. This is a phenomenon known as sarcopenia. Aspects of sarcopenia that are associated with the decrease of muscle mass include increased muscle damage. This is really driven by inflammation. We also see reduced ability to have protein synthesis take place. This is going to result in decrease in muscle building during recovery. Then we also see that our carbohydrate storage, which is referred to as muscle glycogen, that our ability to replenish muscle glycogen after exercise also decreases. We really need to appreciate this physiology and what's going on as we get older if we're going to do a good job with training recovery. The second area I talk about in this article is what are some strategies that we can take given the physiology of aging that's going to help our clients recover more quickly. I just want to talk in the segment today generally about what I explore and that's that we probably need to pay more particular attention to our recovery strategies with older adults. We need to take a holistic approach. In the article I talk about things we can do from an exercise programming standpoint such as always doing active recovery instead of passive recovery. That's going to facilitate blood flow to our muscles and it's going to reduce inflammation. It's going to get those waste products out of there and it's going to bring in important nutrients that's going to drive muscle building and drive muscle glycogen replenishment. Another great exercise programming consideration to be mindful of is that we oftentimes need to take longer between harder sessions. Research has shown for example that following high intensity interval training which can be safely done in older adults we need at least five days of recovery which is a lot longer than the younger adult counterparts. Other strategies that we can take include nutritional strategies. So paying particularly attention to after exercise post-exercise our carbohydrate and our protein ingestion and I give you some specific strategies and specific recommendations in the article. And then a final consideration again being mindful of this holistic approach to training recovery is who doesn't like a massage. Everyone likes a massage and research has shown that massage is great for recovery specifically for older adults. The final thought I want to leave you with is this and that's research for older adults in recovery really remains understudied and sometimes the research is great at telling us things but often times we need to learn from people who are older adults themselves and health and exercise professionals. And I one time several years ago asked a pretty high level older lady who was still completing ultra marathons in her 60s what she did different for training and recovery. She told me something really intriguing. She said my training is really similar except for I do the same amount of training in 16 weeks that I used to do in 12 weeks. === FILE: ACE CERTIFIED ｜ How to Build Self-efficacy and Provide Effective Feedback When Cueing Clients [IYt9Ec2gE24].txt ===

Hi, my name is Chris Gagliardi, I am the scientific education content manager of the American Council on Exercise, and I am also an ACE certified exercise professional and health coach. In this video, I will be talking about self-efficacy and providing effective feedback when cuing clients. Self-efficacy refers to the degree to which an individual believes they can successfully perform a given behavior, skill, or movement, and it can be thought of in terms of beinGHigh or low. When working with clients, you may find that they have a high level of self-efficacy with certain movements, and a low level of self-efficacy with others. For example, a client might have a high level of self-efficacy when it comes to running on a treadmill, while at the same time having a low level of self-efficacy when it comes to weightlifting. The concept of self-efficacy is important to exercise professionals and health coaches because it relates to program adoption and maintenance and improved self-management of chronic disease. Additionally, there is a circular relationship between self-efficacy and behavior change, such that a person's level of self-efficacy is related to whether they will participate in an activity, and a person's participation in an activity influences their self-efficacy level. When it comes to helping clients build self-efficacy, exercise professionals and health coaches play a crucial role by creating positive exercise experiences. One way we can provide a positive exercise experience has to do with the way cueing and feedback is offered. One approach I like to use when providing feedback after a new skill is performed focuses on three things. One, it provides reinforcement for what was done well. Two, it corrects errors. And three, it motivates clients to continue practicing and improving. Essentially, you are sandwiching corrective cues and feedback between reinforcement and motivation. Let's look at an example of how you might use this approach in practice with a participant in a group exercise class. First, you might say, your timing and form were just right on the first five lunges. Here, I am reinforcing what was done well. Next, I would add, remember to keep your back flat even as the exercise starts to feel harder. Here, I am correcting the breakdown in posture that occurred after the fifth repetition. And last, IMight say, learning to maintain correct posture while exercising will make the work feel easier the next time. Here, I am motivating the client to try again by letting them know that the next set may feel easier if they maintain good form. Putting this all together, it will sound like this. Your timing and form were just right on the first five lunges. Remember to keep your back flat even as the exercise starts to feel harder. Next time, keep your back straight and the lunges will feel easier. When providing feedback in the moment, it should be limited to a few simple points and avoid overloading the client with information. While at the same time, correct any errors that need to be addressed. This is especially important when it comes to movement errors impacting client safety. Creating positive exercise experiences will help your clients to build self-efficacy and providing appropriate feedback and cueing is one way that you can support your clients in achieving a high level of belief in their own ability to successfully perform specific exercises. For more information on this topic, check out the April 2024 issue of Certified. Thank you.

Knowing which assessments that you're going to perform, what the protocols are, as well as having the client understand what those protocols are. So not being prepared can introduce a lot of noise with your specific assessment and that can lead to error. Another common issue is not controlling for extraneous variables. So for example, if you're going to perform an assessment of cardiorespiratory fitness, not mentioning to your client that they need to be hydrated, that they need to wear proper clothing, that they need to potentially avoid strenuous exercise the day before, these can all impact the results. And therefore, when you make a baseline assessment and then a reassessment, if there's these extraneous variables also impacting the fitness score that you obtain, that's going to make your interpretation more challenging. And then there's a final issue I want to make you aware of that I don't always see personal trainers and my students understand. And that's that with any physiological measure, there's something called biological variability. Meaning that that parameter is going to change a little bit from day to day, even if we control for all of these extraneous variables. So for example, cardiorespiratory fitness, we know from research that ACE is sponsored in our lab, then that value is going to change about 5% from day to day for the typical individual. And as a result of that, any improvement needs to be above and beyond that 5% score for you and your clients to be confident that there was a true improvement. So those are some considerations when I'm asked the question, what are common issues you see when performing assessments? Last question. Over the past 10 to 20 years, what are some of the biggest changes you've seen on the philosophy regarding assessments? I'm going to give you two. First of all, we know that most participants or most clients goals are to lose weight or make positive changes to their body composition. Unfortunately, research shows that these changes are hard to achieve and take a long time. And as a result of that, one of the more common assessments we used to do is a skin fold assessment with skin fold calipers. And we've really departed from doing that in particular in the last manual that ACE published for personal trainers. And that's because skin fold assessment is notoriously inaccurate. The error can be 3 to 5% even for someone who's very competent. And so typically body composition is actually not going to change by more than a couple of percent even over several months of training. And so we've really tried to discourage using skin fold assessment to evaluate an individual's body composition. And the second big change we've seen in assessment over the last decade or so is that we're trying to really personalize assessments for every client based on their goals. And what I mean by that is at the beginning of an individual's session, what we used to do is we might have a whole battery of assessments that every single client performed regardless of their goals or expectations. And that's not personalizing the assessments. In contrast, more recently to personalize the assessments for each client, we'll sit down and talk to those clients about their goals. And if their goals are to improve balance, then we might do the Y balance test and that's going to be part of their workout. It's not going to be just a separate session just devoted entirely to assessments. So we might just do one assessment within the actual workout or we might just do a couple assessments, maybe the Y balance test to assess balance as well as a walk test to identify the ventilatory threshold one and two. And those can be two things that are embedded into the first session made to be more like a workout instead of a just a series of assessments. And so personalizing the workout or personalizing the assessments is one big change. And then the other is, as I referred to, really discouraging use of the skin fold assessment.

=== FILE: ACE CERTIFIED ｜ How to Help Clients Get Back on Track After Taking a Break From Training [-GnDZgBeEEg].txt ===

Hi, my name is Chris Gagliardi. I am the scientific education content manager of the American Council on Exercise. And I'm also an A-certified personal trener, group fitness instructor, health coach, and medical exercise specialist. In this video, I will be talking about a principle of exercise trening known as reversibility. The principle of reversibility suggests that any improvement in physical fitness due to physical activity is entirely reversible with the discontinuation of the trening program. In other words, when it comes to physical fitness, we must use it or we will lose it. If we use our muscles, they can increase in size and strength relatively quickly, regardless of our age. But if we don't use them, they'll become smaller and weaker. In fact, an age-sponsored research study set out to investigate just how quickly fitness fades. The research revealed that after completing a 13-week trening program, if physical activity is stopped, then all trening adaptations will be completely lost within one month. Perhaps you have experienced the principle of reversibility yourself, or can imagine how frustrating this would be for a client. You worked so hard to improve your health and fitness, and then all the benefits you achieved could be lost after not exercising for a month. This can be devastating and disheartening. At the same time, this reinforces the importance of physical activity as a component of a healthy lifestyle, rather than as a short-term process for attaining a temporary objective. It is our role as health coaches and exercise professionals to not only support clients to get moving, but also to help clients to develop skills, uncover the motivation, and to develop the self-efficacy to keep moving for the long-term. So, how can we help? While not all lapses in trening can be avoided, especially if they are the result of injury or illness, there is much we can do to support our clients in staying on the right track, or help them to find their way back to the path they were on. Here are three steps you can follow using the ACE Mover method to reassure your clients that even though lapses in trening may occur, with proper planning, you can minimize the effects of detrening and move forward. Step one, ask powerful open-ended questions. You can use open-ended questions to find out more from your client about what led to the setback in their trening. After asking a powerful question, be sure to use active listening skills and remember to respond without judgment. This is the time to be curious and to listen to your clients who are the experts on themselves and should be viewed as being resourceful and capable of change. SomEQuestions you might initially ask include, what events took place that led you to stop trening? What differences have you noticed in the way you feel now that you are not exercising? What barriers got in the way of your physical activity plan? Or, how important is it for you to get back on track with your physical activity goals? Step two, break down barriers. This involves asking morEQuestions to discover what potential barriers may get in the way of sticking with an exercise plan. The answers to thesEQuestions can help to establish a game plan for the next time goal-threatening situations occur. SomEQuestions you might ask at this point include, what will you do differently the next time you face similar barriers? What other barriers to exercise do you anticipate? And, what do you need to start doing now to get back on track? Step three, collaborate on next steps. This final step includes summarizing what was learned in steps one and two and then letting the client decide how they would like to move forward. In this step, allow the client to lead the discussion and take care to ensure a smart goal and next steps are established. Questions you can ask here include, what will you do? How would you like to move forward? And, what is one thing you can start doing today to get back on track? Step backs can't always be avoided, but many of the barriers that get in the way of consistently achieving our goals can be planned for. It is important to remind your clients that doing something is better than doing nothing when it comes to being physically active. Even if you can't do your planned activity for the day, you can still be active. Work together with your clients to develop strategies to remain active even during inevitable program breaks. Thank you for watching.

We found that in going through the ABC step in the process of breaking down barriers, some participants needed 30 minutes to go through that ABC step. In other instances when we started talking about breaking down barriers, a participant needed to think about that and they would come in the next exercise session and say I thought about some of those barriers and I'd like to discuss those with you now. And so the take-home message on this is it's really important as we as exercise professionals apply the ACE mover method and the ABC approach we realize that not every client is going to be the same in terms of how long it takes to go through that and if it even occurs in the same session. The next really intriguing finding that we we found in our project was that there was considerable variability in terms of how much time we spent on a given lifestyle behavior with the ACE mover method approach. Our general approach was we wanted to try to get participants to eat better make healthier choices in terms of their their eating habits. We wanted to also reduce stress and we wanted to help them limit sedentary behavior. What we what we found is that a given participant might have been able to do that. A given participant might need just a single session to go through some some strategies for limiting their sedentary time and then they were good to move on the next week to another lifestyle behavior. Whereas another participant might need three weeks four weeks or or even a month and a half to talk about some of their barriers for limiting their sedentary time. That was really interesting and I think very important to share with you as exercise professionals. Another consideration was that some participants just got really motivated and they wanted to talk about modifying a couple lifestyle habits at the same time. So they were like hey I want to I want to eat better and I want to reduce my stress and I want to talk about that this week during this ABC approach. And at first we were a little reluctant to try to do that and in fact in some instances when it was really obvious that it would overwhelm a given participant to try and tackle too many lifestyle changes at the same time. We really tried to encourage them that hey let's first talk about healthier lifestyle choices and then next we'll talk about stress reduction. We'll come back to that. In other instances though where it really seemed like as we went through asking these open-ended questions and they said yeah I think I can eat better and reduce stress. I can do that at the same time and what are the barriers and some of those barriers were the same. And as we collaborated it was really apparent that hey let's do both at the same time. So again some really interesting insights in terms of how the ACE ABC approach and the ACE mover method approach works in different clients. So in closing it's really my strong belief that exercise professionals can translate some of these insights we found from the research into making profound impacts in the lives of their clients. And really help assist them to positively change their health related behaviors. So once again it's great to always talk with you in these short video segments that accompany the articles in ACE certified. And once again Happy New Year.

ceroleum and tricephalogram, power하는성, E2GDTEXT The barbell clen and jerk is a total-body exercise that uses the hips, the legs, the core and the shoulders. There are four phases to the barbell clen and jerk. There's the first pull off the floor, the second pull, the catch and the overhead press or the jerk action driving the barbell overhead. Start with the first pull. You want to engage the hips, press the feet into the floor and snap the hips forward in order to start the upper momentum of the barbell. At the second pull, the barbell is passing the knees in front of the mid-thighs and it's very important to explosively pop the hips forward in order to generate the upper momentum for the barbell. The upper body doesn't do that much work to lift the bar off the floor. All the work is done from the hips. For the catch, the lifter has to drop his or her body below the bar in order to receive it right across the front of the shoulders. Once they've caught the bar in the front of the shoulders, they want to return to a full standing upright position. Both feet should be about a hip width apart. Knees should be soft. You want to have the bar right across the front of the shoulders with the palms up and the elbows pointing straight ahead. For the overhead jerk phase, it's important that once the lifter generates the upward momentum of the barbell, that he or she can cycle their legs to land in a lunge position and sink their body underneath the bar. With the right leg in front and the left leg back in the bar overhead, the lifter wants to press the right foot into the ground as you swing the left foot forward in order to come to a full standing upright position. It's recommended that you want to step underneath the weight moving forward as opposed to stepping backwards with the front leg. That's to be avoided. From a standing position with the arms overhead and feet hip width apart, you want to teach the lifter how to slowly bring the bar back to the shoulders and then down to the hips and return it to the floor. Remember, an extremely important thing about the barbell clen and jerk is that this is a power-based exercise. The body will fatigue after only three or four repetitions because so many muscles are involved. So when teaching this lift, focus on form, focus on technique, not volume. Don't be intimidated by this exercise. Be smart about teaching the barbell clen and jerk. For more health and fitness information, visit acefitness.org.

=== FILE: ACE CERTIFIED ｜ Master the Deadlift [dQiBlIk4dfE].txt ===

anterior spinal muscles, posterior finger reflex, anterior cilia, anterior lower jaw flexion, anterior end of patreon! The dead lift is an excellent exercise for both men and women who want to work on total body strength and work on total core strength as well. When you look at the dead lift, you really have to get a lot of hip mobility, you sink back into your hips to perform a hit pinch and then you have to work the deep stabilizers around the spine. The dead lift may sound like an intimidating name, but the name dead lift cum from the fact that the weight is sitting dead and not moving on the floor. When teaching the dead lift, use the barbell. If you don't have a standard size 45-pound plate, you can put smaller plates on risers or put the bar on an appropriate rack that keeps the bar off the floor. As a client improves their strength and their form in the dead lift, you can increase the weight and move the weight lower, closer to the floor. One important aspect of the dead lift is taking an over-under grip. Having one palm supinated up and one palm pronated down gives you a strong grip that can keep the bar rolling out from your grasp. There are three phases to the dead lift, the setup, the pull, and the lockout. For the setup, approach the bar resting on the floor. If you want to have the bar touching your shins as you place your feet firmly on the floor, you want to hinge back into your hips, keep a nice, long, extended spine, and sink into your hips as you reach down to grab the bar and that over-under grip. To lift the weight, you want to initiate the pull phase of the movement. Keep your spine straight, keep your chest lifted, and push your feet into the floor like you're trying to push the floor away from you. As you push your feet into the floor during the pull, imagine pushing your hips forward at the same time as pulling your knees back. That will engage the entire range of motion of the hamstrings and the glutes to help work on hip extension. At the top during the lockout, lift your chest, set your shoulders, and make sure you engage your lats in order to keep the bar stable. To return the weight to the floor, simply push your weight back into the hips and reverse the hinge and slowly, under control, bring the weight back down to the ground. It's recommended that your client take the time to reset between each repetition. If necessary, reset the feet and reset the grip. When teaching the deadlift, it's very important to work on the fundamentals and the skills of the movement. Any exercise is a movement, and movement is a skill that must be taught properly. If you ever see your client start to round the spine, drop their shoulders, or lift with their back, you want to stop immediately and maybe either raise the bar up off the ground so you don't have to go as low, or use a lighter weight. You really want to focus on teaching the skill, and then once your client learns the skill of the deadlift, you'll find they'll be able to progress quickly. One benefit of the deadlift is that because you're using so much muscle tissue, you're going to burn a lot of calories, which if your client has weight loss goals, that's an extra benefit. If your client has aesthetic goals, then the deadlift uses the glutes, the hamstring, and inner thigh muscles, so it's a great way to work on the aesthetics of the lower body. For more information about the deadlift, or to find out more about any other exercise, go to www.acefitness.org.

=== FILE: ACE ProSource Technique Series： Turkish Get Up [6GtRx5on7wg].txt ===

everyone. The Turkish get-up is a very important exercise to consider adding to your client's program. It's a perfect combination of stability, mobility, and strength all in one movement. When teaching the Turkish get-up you want to have your client lying on the floor, they want to lie next to the weight, grip it with both hands and roll from their side onto their back. They want to extend both arms overhead and firmly grip the kettlebell and keep the right arm extended as they lower their left arm and extend it out to their side. The right knee should be bent and the right foot should be flat on the ground. From the lying position with the left arm out to the side, have your client keep the right arm extended as they roll up onto their left elbow. Once they're on their left elbow, they lift their chest and extend their spine and come up to their left hand. With their left hand on the ground and the left leg out in front of their body and the right foot firmly planted on the ground, teach them how to push their right foot into the ground as they lift both hips into the air. It's very important to teach this bridge position with the left leg extended and the right foot on the ground with the right arm overhead. From the bridge position, coach your client to push their right foot into the ground as they sweep the left leg back underneath their body and come up onto their left knee in a kneeling position. From a kneeling position, have them maintain a nice, tall, long, extended spine as they push their right foot into the ground and come into a standing position. At the standing position, pause for a second, extend the spine, and all you're going to do is go in reverse and have your clients learn how to do this in reverse. Sometimes one teaching technique can actually be teaching it from the standing position down to the lying position to help them learn the sequence of the movement. Holding this bridge position is important on the way back down to teach them how to properly engage their glutes and their core to maintain stability. So with the left hand on the ground, the left leg extended in front of the body, and the right foot pushed into the ground, slowly lower the hips until you come into a seated position. With your left hand on the ground, slowly roll your left elbow onto the floor and start lying back onto your spine to return to the full lying position while maintaining the right arm overhead. When your client is lying on the ground with the arm extended overhead and they're finished with the exercise, have them slowly bring the weight down to their chest with both hands and roll over to the side and place the weight out in front of them. From there, they can get up and move on to the next exercise. One very important technique for learning this movement is to coach their client to look up their hand during the exercise. Your body follows your eyes. If your client's eyes are on the mirror in front of them, they won't learn how to properly sequence the movement because they won't think about what their body is actually doing. So if you're looking for a great way to help your clients work on their total body strength and burn calories at the same time, consider adding the Turkish Get Up to their program. For more health and fitness information, go to acefitness.org.

=== FILE: ACE Youth Fitness ABCs of Fitness Training [x8awpP-5WlQ].txt ===

the ABCs of physical activity are so critical to implement for kids on a daily basis. Sometimes it might seem difficult to implement all the different aspects that are important for a nice, full, well-rounded fitness program. You want to incorporate locomotor control, stability control, and even object control. The latter's a great way to get kids to focus in on the movements and the skills they're learning. It gives them some direction and something to shoot for. So I want you to start with two feet inside the square. You're going to do jumping jacks all the way down to the end. So feet go apart, and then together. Popping off the balls of the feet, working on some strength, it's a very easy pattern. They're not really thinking about the exercise they're doing. They're thinking about the goal of achieving that movement, which makes it much more attainable for kids to do. That's why the latter is such a fun tool. Now the tennis ball exercise is so great because you have to learn reaction time, a little bit of throwing, catching. Learning that at a young age is so critical because you're developing those patterns that kids can use throughout their entire life. These drills not only help these kids get stronger, faster, quicker, but throughout life they'll be healthier human beings because they've attained these different skills at a young age.

=== FILE: Behavior Change Specialist Program [u8Kw4czgwkA].txt ===

 If we're going to have an impact on chronic disease, we've got to learn how to help people make those small but incredibly challenging lifestyle behavioral changes. One of the things that we recognize is that we really need to train our professionals, professionals to understand how to help people make lifestyle behavioral changes. To really take a much deeper dive into the kind of nuts and bolts of coaching people through behavior change. Working with individuals like Bill Miller who really is kind of considered the father of motivational interviewing. James Prochaska who most people are familiar with the trans theoretical model of behavior change, the stages of change. We worked with Robert Bisward Diener who is kind of considered the leading expert with regard to positive psychology. Worked with an experimental psychologist from Stanford, BJ Fogg who has the tiny habits program really is kind of this small steps to behavior change. We thought it was important in the manual to have a number of interactive exercises baked into each of the chapters so that when we're having a discussion about positive psychology, what are some practical exercises that we could have to participate in to help them really kind of cement those skills. Through these interactive coaching demonstrations where they serve as the coach working with a cohort of their peers and they are evaluated by a master coach on their abilities to actually put into practice what they've learned. And it also allows them to get expert feedback and advice from a trained coach with a lot of history and knowledge with regard to conducting a motivational interviewing session or helping an individual with appropriate goal setting. And so you're able to kind of get this feedback, use it to kind of refine and tweak your techniques so that you have the confidence that you've not only learned about all these wonderful things that are taught in the curriculum, but you've actually done them. And given the nature of the content, this program has received recognition and approval for continuing education from a number of allied health care organizations. Most leading health care and allied health organizations recognize that being able to help people make lifestyle behavioral changes is going to be one of the most impactful things in reversing some of these disturbing trends. So there are going to be an opportunity and an increasing need for individuals who are well skilled in helping people make such changes.

=== FILE: CERTIFIED ｜ ACE-SUPPORTED RESEARCH： Does the ACE Integrated Fitness Training® Model Really Work？ [T2PrFYYVzAk].txt ===

Hi there, I'm Sabrina Jo, ACE's Senior Director of Science and Research. Over the pasT3 years, ACE has supported ongoing research investigating the effectiveness of the ACE Integrated Fitness trening Model, or ACE-IFT model, which is a personalized approach to exercise that optimizes trening responsiveness and impacts cardiorespiratory and muscular fitness as well as cardiometabolic health. In our certified article on the year one research, you can read about previous studies investigating the ACE-IFT model and the ACE mover method and watch a video on how to successfully apply the muscular trening component of the model with your clients. In the year two article, you can read about the importance of muscular fitness for a person's overall health. In this video, I'm going to summarize the findings of the year three research and explore the overall findings of this multi-year, multi-site study. The study conducted by Dr. Lance Dalek and his team at Western Colorado University sought to determine if the ACE-IFT model was more effective than standardized trening. 31 physically active men and women aged between 18 and 64 participated in this 13-week study. They were divided into three groups, the ACE-IFT model group, the standardized trening group, and the control group. Both the ACE-IFT model and the standardized trening groups underwent cardiorespiratory and muscular trening with the ACE-IFT model group receiving a program based on their individual ventilatory thresholds. Now, on to the results. The ACE-IFT model group saw more desirable changes in body mass, waist circumference, body fat percentage, VO2 max, and strength. They also saw significant improvement in systolic blood pressure, HDL cholesterol, and blood glucose. In short, the personalized approach of the ACE-IFT model drove more dramatic improvements across multiple measures. The most meaningful outcome is the consistency of these findings over three years, supporting the effectiveness and personalized exercise programming using the ACE-IFT model. This means that the findings of any one of the studies aren't a fluke because these benefits were found across three different studies and groups of participants. So what can exercise professionals take away from this study? Here are three actionable tips. First, emphasize personalized programming. Every individual is unique, so their fitness program should be too. Use your client's individual physiology and goals to tailor their program. Two, balance cardiorespiratory and muscular trening. The study showed the importance of both cardiorespiratory and muscular trening in promoting overall health. And third, keep an eye on cardiometabolic health. Don't forget about waist circumference, triglycerides, HDL cholesterol, blood pressure, and blood glucose. These are key indicators of overall health and can be greatly improved with personalized exercise programming. There you have it. Remember, the key to optimal fitness outcomes is a personalized approach. Thanks for watching this video.

=== FILE: CERTIFIED ｜ ACE-Supported Research： Can This Program Help More Americans Be Physically Active？ [1s7GUa5mnOU].txt ===

Hi there, I'm Sabrina Jo, ACE's Senior Director of Science and Research. We know that the fitness industry is not reaching roughly 75% of the population, so thEQuestion must be asked, how do we reach those individuals and communities who may not be able to afford our services or don't have easy access to the appropriate resources? Today I want to share with you a little information abouT3 Winds Fitness, which is one of ACE's community engagement partners. Three Winds Fitness is recognized as an innolvative program for delivering positive physical activity experiences to populations and communities that may not otherwise have access to such programs. The concept behind Three Winds Fitness is simple. University students studying kinesiology volunteer to lead fitness classes every morning in parks throughout the surrounding area. Everyone is welcome, the program is free, and participants may choose from three different classes designed to meet people where they are in their physical activity journeys. What makes Three Winds Fitness unique is its creative approach to the meeting the needs of not only community members but also university students through a partnership among academia, local municipalities, nonprofit organizations, and the business community. So what are the three winds that the program promises? Number one is participant health, number two is community health, and number three is student professional development. The goal of a recent ACE-sponsored research project was to determine how effective the program is at meeting these stated objectives. The study consisted of nine focus groups with a total of 51 participants, including current and past student instructors, and participants spanning the full 10-year period from when Three Winds Fitness was launched. Three themes emerged during the analysis following the focus group sessions. The first was defined as the perceived value or benefit from the program. What they found was that the program provided both professional and personal benefits to the student instructors as well as benefits to the participants and the community at large. The second theme was health, where the Three Winds Fitness program was shown to positively impact the health of the participants and the community. Finally, the third theme was social connection, which manifested in several ways. The student instructors reported feeling a strong sense of community and support with their fellow instructors as well as with the participants. In addition, the participants felt like they were becoming more connected to their communities. Stated simply, this research found that the Three Winds Fitness program delivers the wins it promises. What makes these findings particularly exciting is the potential public health impact of programs like this one. ACE's mission is to get people moving, and ACE-certified professionals are doing public health work each and every day as they educate people about how to eat more healthfully, move more often, and make the behavior changes that empower them to live healthier lifestyles. Programs like Three Winds Fitness can serve as a model for how to improve the health of those communities that need it the most through free programming that also offers important benefits for the instructors who lead the exercise sessions. The results of this research should serve as a call to action to everyone from public health policymakers to individual exercise professionals and health coaches. Three Winds Fitness is a model that works, is free To the public, has proven benefits to both the participants and instructors, and is a scalable, replicable, and cost-effective program. It's time to get involved and help more people get moving and enjoy the benefits of a more active lifestyle. Thanks for watching.

And some of the main problems when clients with MS get overheated is that they can have more fatigue or undue fatigue than is actually usual. And so they're already dealing with that as a really common symptom. It just makes it worse. You know, another problem can be blurred vision. And so I've had clients with MS who developed blurred vision while they were exercising just because we weren't careful enough. === FILE: CERTIFIED ｜ Exercise Programming for Clients Who Have Arthritis [uWYT7ysgB0Q].txt ===

Hi, I'm Lance Dallek, professor of exercise and sports science at Western Colorado University and also a member of the ACE scientific advisory panel. In this month's issue of Certified, Angela Dallek and myself have authored an article on programming considerations for clients with osteoarthritis and rheumatoid arthritis. Arthritis is a very common clinical condition. One in five individuals in the United States is impacted. And in this article, there's a variety of programming considerations that we present to you, including if your individual clients with arthritis also has a comorbidity or multiple comorbidities such as diabetes and heart disease, considerations for the medications that clients with arthritis are prescribed and taking, the various exercise considerations that you need to be mindful of for working with your clients with arthritis. In this video segment though, I want to focus on that initial few weeks of programming for clients with arthritis and what we have tended to focus on and what we think is critical for a successful long-term collaboration. There's three things. One is to, prior to really jumping into exercise, is focusing on range of motion activities. Next is to progress slowly. And the third consideration is we want to build trust. How do we do all of these three things in the first several weeks and what does that look like and what do we need to be thinking about? We oftentimes have some research available and we share with our clients before we start with range of motion activities that it's anticipated that over the first few sessions there's going to actually be an increase in pain and sickness. And clients with arthritis can be fearful of this, but what we want to share is that that's just going to last for a few days or a week and then it's going to get better. And when you can actually demonstrate that after performing range of motion activities for a week or so that stiffness lessens, pain is reduced, your clients are going to trust you. Some other things we're trying to accomplish with these range of motion activities is, as I've already alluded to a little bit, we're trying to reduce stiffness, we're trying to reduce pain, we're trying to improve posture. And by establishing this at baseline over the first few weeks, as we progress with functional exercises and cardiorespiratory and muscular trening exercises, those are all going to have a much more likelihood for a beneficial response and tolerated well for our clients if we focused on better posture, reduced stiffness. With range of motion activities, some other things to be thinking about and things that we've outlined in this article are to progress slowly, finding that sweet spot where there's going to be a little bit of increased pain and stiffness initially, but that's going to get better, and recognizing that the side effects of stiffness and pain will come about a day or two later after the preliminary range of motion activities, and so we want to really be careful in seeinGHow our clients are responding and not doing too much too fast. Also finding various range of motion activities that clients can perform throughout the full range of motion around a given joint, and if they can't, to find a different particular activity. So to reiterate, what we focus on and what we think is really important is range of motion, progressing slowly, and building trust. And then with the range of motion activities, building a consistency of doing this on a daily basis, even as they start up with their exercise, and really trying to avoid a starting and stopping pattern, because doing this with clients with arthritis tends to feed into this negative loop of more stiffness and more pain as you start up with more activity, and then when you become inactive, that pain and stiffness returns, but building consistency can really help manage those symptoms, which are really symptoms that are exhibited in most of the arthritis clients that we've worked with over the years. So in closing, good luck with your clients, and we'll see you next time. Thank you.

=== FILE: CERTIFIED ｜ Exercise Programming for Clients Who Have Asthma [_c51-8cY9WU].txt ===

Hi, my name is Lance Dallek, professor of exercise and sports science at Western Colorado University, and also a member of the ACE scientific advisory panel. In this month's issue of Certified, I've written an article on exercise programming considerations for individuals with asthma and or exercise induced bronchoconstriction, or EIB for short as an acronym. And in this video segment, I want to talk about how as a health and exercise professional, I employ the dose response relationship concept to managing clients with asthma and or EIB. First, let's define a dose response relationship. A dose response relationship refers to a dose of a factor and how changes in the dose of that factor elicits a corresponding response and a varying corresponding response. So as an example, we know that there is a dose response relationship between exercise volume and health benefits. For example, as you go from 30 to 60 minutes per week, five days per week, you get a corresponding favorable response in improved cardiorespiratory fitness levels. Well, that same dose response relationship and the concept can be applied to how we can prevent individuals with asthma and or EIB from experiencing symptoms. There's really three factors that we want to consider in terms of a dose response relationship in managing symptoms of asthma and or EIB. The first is the concentration of an allergen in the environment. So for example, a higher concentration of a pollen count for an individual means that there's going to be an increased risk for experiencing symptoms such as coughing or wheezing, shortness of breath. And so understanding that dose response relationship between higher dose of pollen count and an unfavorable response is important in terms of managing symptoms or limiting symptoms that an individual client may have with asthma and or exercise-induced bronchoconstriction. And in the article, I've included a link to a pollen forecast website that can be used. The second factor in terms of a dose response relationship is that as ventilation rate increases, there can ultimately be an increased response in terms of symptoms of asthma and or EIB. And so being able to identify or pinpoint what that threshold is is important so that you can stay below a threshold of increased ventilation. And ventilation can be driven by the duration of exercise and or the intensity. And so it can be kind of a combination. So if your exercise intensity is low, your ventilation rate may need to persist for a longer duration before you experience some of these symptoms. Whereas if the intensity is high, the ventilation rate is high, the number of minutes you need to exercise may be a lot lower before you reach that critical threshold. So the third factor there is the interplay between intensity and duration of exercise and understanding what that is. So as a, again, an exercise and health professional, when I'm working with my clients, for example, with exercise-induced bronchoconstriction, IMay be looking at what, with this dose response relationship concept in mind, I'm looking at, well, how long can this individual exercise before their ventilation, their total ventilation rate, and that's accumulated to the point where they start to become experiencing symptoms. Again, they're getting shortness of breath, they start coughing, and I'm trying to manage that with exercise program design. And then similarly, I'm taking a client who has asthma. We know what their allergens are, we know what their stimuli are, and we're looking at that pollen forecast website in our geographical region, and we're saying, ah, the concentration is too high. You shouldn't exercise outside on these days. You should be in an indoor environment. So in summary, as I've outlined in the article, a lot of the exercise programming considerations for individuals with asthma and or exercise-induced bronchoconstriction are actually quite identical to what they would be in an otherwise normal, healthy population with some various considerations. But a big approach I've taken, which I've outlined in this video clip, is this dose response concept, which factors to pay attention to, concentration of allergens, ventilation rate, and ventilation rate determined by duration of exercise as well as intensity of exercise. So as always, it's great to talk to you. I hope you enjoy this month's article, and good luck with your clients, and we'll see you next time.

=== FILE: CERTIFIED ｜ Exercise and Ageism： 4 Strategies for Working With Older Adults [9d-AOkd_1_g].txt ===

Hi there, I'm Sabrina Jo, ACE's Senior Director of Science and Research. My number one tip for successfully working with older adults is to never assume that you know what they need simply because of their age. Now obviously we shouldn't assume what any client needs regardless of how old they are, but I've found that in the business of fitness it's common for treners and coaches to treat clients who are middle-aged and older differently because they think that programming for senior folks tends to be a one-size-fits-all formula and nothing could be further from the truth. To help with this ACE has recently published two articles that cover some excellent strategies for working with older adults. The first article discusses the effects of aging on physical activity. For example, the impact of aging on collagen production can lead to decreased elasticity which could result in injury followed by increased healing time which can cause lapses in activity. Because of this exercise professionals should understand the risk and reward of exercise selection for each client as well as the importance of recovery time. It is also essential to consider chronic conditions that may impact motivation and professionals should help clients practice self-compassion and adapt to changes in their physical abilities. The second article emphasizes the importance of communication and personalized exercise programming when working with older adults and then provides several strategies for developing exercise programs for older clients. These strategies include starting slowly and progressing safely, focusing on recovery, offering options to adapt movements and being mindful of unique aging characteristics. Starting slowly and progressing safely is a must for any client. It's erroneous to think that older adults can't tren to get stronger and fitter because they absolutely can and many often have increased fitness and performance as goals. Applying safe progression principles and having open communication about how workouts are feeling for older clients will help determine appropriate intensity and pacing. Focusing on recovery both throughout the week and within each session will help reduce the risk of injury. Often clients can perform the same types of activities they did when they were younger but simply need more time to recover. When you offer movement options for any exercise it will free you to be more creative in your programming as you work with clients to find what feels right for them. Lastly, being mindful of unique aging characteristics means that you can personalize exercise programs that take into account the client's capabilities and limitations. For example, issues related to aging such as memory, health and fitness status, disease status and self-efficacy all play a part in safe and effective program design. Ultimately to retain clients safety is essential but effective programming should also be considered. The overarching goal is to empower clients to make continual progress without pushing them beyond their limits which could lead to injury or illness. Thanks for watching this video. you

Our motivations or reasons for behaving a certain way can change from day to day, from week to week, or within any time frame, depending on our circumstances, and the way we perceive what is most important to us at a particular time? For instance, one day your motivation for change may be to become more fit, and then the next day you wake up with back pain, and your motivation for changing shifts to eliminating pain. Motivation is fluid and changes depending on what is happening within and around us. So what can we do about motivation? The ACE Mover Method is a fantastic way to use open-ended questions and collaborative conversation to learn more about what motivates the client. The ACE Mover Method is a client-centered approach that can be used to empower clients to make behavioral changes to improve their health, fitness, and overall quality of life by taking a client-centered approach and recognizing that clients are the foremost experts on themselves, using powerful open-ended questions and active listening during every client session, and by genuinely believing that your clients are resourceful and capable of change. The ACE Mover Method can be applied by using the ACE ABC approach to ask open-ended questions, break down barriers, and to collaborate on goals and next steps. Not only can the Mover Method and ACE ABC approach be used to help build the client professional relationship and rapport, but it is also a great way to learn more about what is motivating a client to change while continuing to position the client as an active partner in their behavior change journey. Let's explore an example of how you can put all of this together. You've been working with a client for six months. Initially, they were motivated to change their behavior to control their weight and improve their fitness. However, during a recent session, they mentioned feeling stressed out for the past few months and being mentally fatigued at work. After using good active listening skills, you decide to ask the following open-ended question. What has changed in your life that is leading you to feel more stressed and mentally fatigued? The client then proceeds to tell you about some family obligations, changing work and school schedules, and some large projects they are leading at work. Sound familiar? After listening to understand these new developments in your client's life, you ask another open-ended question to help identify any barriers to managing stress. What strategies are you currently using to help manage your stress and how effective you perceive those strategies to be? The client then proceeds to tell you that they are not doing anything to manage their stress levels and that they are just accepting the fact that they will be stressed out as part of life. However, they also mention that they would love to get their stress level under control and continue to improve their fitness. At this point, we know that the client would like to learn more about stress management and is still interested in improving their fitness. However, it currently appears that managing stress is a priority above improving fitness levels. But can we do both? It feels like another good opportunity for an open-ended question. So, managing your stress and improving your fitness are both important goals for you. What do you already know about how these things work together? We've already learned that a barrier for the client is not knowing how to manage stress. And with this question, we open the door to using the Elicit Provide Elicit approach to find out what the client already knows about the topic, to share relevant information, and to make sure they understand the information that you shared. All this new information indicates that stress management has become a greater motivating factor and can be used to collaborate on next steps, modify your approach, identify support, provide accountability, shift session focus, and adjust measures of success to align with your client's motivation for change. Remember, the greatest impact that health coaches and exercise professionals can have on the lives of their clients is to help them positively change health-related behaviors and to establish positive relationships with those new behaviors. Check in with your clients regularly about their motivation for change and use active listening to discover how their motivation may be shifting. To learn more about this topic, please check out the Shifting Priorities Certified article. Thank you.

=== FILE: CERTIFIED ｜ Program Hopping vs. Proper Progressions [xK7s3rRY7EE].txt ===

Hi, I'm Pete McCall for the American Council on Exercise to talk for a minute about program hopping. Social media makes it seem like giving a client a different workout every day is a great idea. See a client on a Monday, they do one workout, see them on a Thursday, they do a different workout. Now, if you're following a consistent program, you might have like a push day on a Monday, you might have a pull day on a Thursday, that's one thing. But when you're designing workouts for clients, you're giving them a different workout each time and there's no progression, there's no motor learning going on, there's no skill development, there's no real progression. Keep in mind, exercise is movement and movement is a skill that must be practiced. You want to practice coordinated movement patterns with clients. What IMean by that is the movement patterns of pushing, pulling, rotating, hinging, squatting, lunging, carrying. Movement patterns use multiple muscle groups at the same time. When we do that, what we do is we increase caloric expenditure. Also, what's happening is we're trening the brain how to activate multiple muscles at the same time. That's why it's so important to be consistent for a period of four, six, or eight weeks, anywhere between four and eight weeks. Being consistent with exercises will help clients learn the movement pattern and learn the skill. That's where we see true progression coming in. If all we're doing is giving clients a different workout every time we see them, we won't have an opportunity to see them improve at doing a particular exercise or doing a particular movement. If you have a workout based on movement, if you have a workout based on the movement pattern for your clients, we need to stay consistent with that. It should be a period of time. Practice the movements. As they get better, load the movements. Give them a heavier object. Challenge them. Be explosive. Be dynamic, but keep the patterns the same. Now, here's an idea. If you want to make the workout a little bit more interesting for some clients, here's all you got to do. Maybe one day you do goblet squats with a kettlebell. Maybe another day you do goblet squats with a dumbbell. Same pattern, different tool. Maybe one day you do standing cable rows with a cable machine like this. Maybe the next workout you do standing bent over rows. Same pattern, different tool. So that's the idea. When you design programs for your clients, you want to stay consistent with the movement patterns because that's going to help them develop better coordination and better movement skill. That's a true sign of regression. But if all you do is give a client a different workout every time you see them, they don't get a chance to practice a particular exercise. They don't get a chance to improve a particular skill. So when designing exercise programs for clients, be consistent with the application of the skill and you can adjust the tools to give them a slightly different experience. I'm Pete from the American Council on Exercise. Hopefully that gives you some great ideas for how you can optimize your client's workout programs.

 nassin, nassin, When we look at how individuals appear on a continuum relative to their fitness and functional capabilities, we really start with function, move into health, progress to fitness, and then ultimately into performance.
